## 1 **Abstract**

2 In the present study, we investigated the induced systemic resistance (ISR) activated by 3 the beneficial fungus *Trichoderma atroviride* in maize plants, and the early 4 immunological responses triggered after challenge with the ear rot pathogen *Fusarium*  5 *verticillioides.* By transcriptional analysis, we were able to identify the gene core set 6 specifically modulated in silks of maize plants expressing ISR. Our results showed that 7 the main transcriptional reprogramming falls into genes involved in five main functional 8 categories: cell structure/cell wall, amino acid and protein metabolism, stress responses, 9 signaling, and transport. Among these ISR-related genes, it is important to highlight 10 novel findings regarding hormone metabolism and signaling. The expression of 11 hormone-dependent genes was in good agreement with the ABA, JA and SA levels 12 detected in the plants under study. The experimental design allowed the identification of 13 novel regulatory elements related to a heightened state of defense in silks and suggests 14 that steroids and SA are central components of a master regulatory network controlling 15 the immunity of silks during ISR. The results presented also provide evidence about the 16 molecular mechanisms used by maize silks against *F. verticilliodes* to counteract 17 pathogenic development and host invasion, including PR genes, plant cell-wall 18 reinforcement, fungal cell-wall degrading enzymes and secondary metabolism.

19

## 20 **Keywords:**

# 21 *Fusarium***, Induced Systemic Resistance, Hormone Signaling, Maize, Regulatory** 22 **Elements,** *Trichoderma.*

#### 1 **Introduction**

2 Pathogenic diseases of maize crop have a strong impact on the global economy, 3 especially in areas where this cereal is a primary food commodity. The fungus 4 *Fusarium verticillioides* (Sacc.) Nirenberg (synonym *F. moniliforme* Sheldon, 5 teleomorph *Gibberella moniliformis* Wineland), which is endemic in maize fields at 6 harvest, is the main causal agent of maize ear rot (Chulze *et al.*, 1996; Bottalico, 1998; 7 Logrieco *et al.*, 2002; Battilani *et al.*, 2008; Folcher *et al.*, 2009). This pathogen can 8 cause not only ear rotting but also produce massive amounts of mycotoxins belonging 9 to the fumonisin family, which have deleterious effects on humans and farm animals.

10 At the silking stage, *F. verticillioides* invades the ear through the silk channel and 11 colonizes the developing kernels via a narrow opening called the stylar canal (Koehler, 12 1942; Duncan and Howard, 2010). As colonization continues, the host plant triggers 13 several defense mechanisms in an attempt to stop the infection process (Lanubile *et al.*, 14 2012; Ferrigo *et al.*, 2014; Lanubile *et al.*, 2014).

15 Since no maize line displaying full resistance to ear rot disease has yet been identified, 16 breeding may be a the safest and most and sustainable strategy to reduce *F.*  17 *verticillioides* incidence in maize fields (Munkvold, 2003; Clements et al., 2004; Eller et 18 al., 2008; Lanubile et al., 2010; Lanubile et al., 2017). A heightened state of resistance 19 can be achieved as the outcome of the plant interaction with beneficial microorganisms 20 that enhance immune responses and reduce pathogenic impact (Pieterse et al., 2014; 21 Schlaeppi and Bulgarelli, 2015). Many species of rhizobacteria and fungi have been 22 identified as beneficial for plant growth and integrity (PGPR and PGPF, respectively) 23 (Pieterse *et al.*, 2014). Thus, the use of these microorganisms to further investigate plant 24 immune responses may represent a valuable resource to expose hidden molecular 25 mechanisms essential to reduce disease development.

1 In this context, the genus *Trichoderma* includes many non-pathogenic soilborne (free-2 living) filamentous fungal species able to colonize plant roots as avirulent symbionts 3 (Harman *et al.*, 2004). Several *Trichoderma* species have been proved to be antagonist 4 agents able to control fungal pathogens, oomycetes and nematodes (Druzhinina *et al.*, 5 2011). In addition, members of this fungal genus have been demonstrated to be plant 6 growth promoters, improving and maintaining soil productivity, and thus providing 7 important benefits to agriculture (Hermosa *et al.*, 2013). *Trichoderma* species can also 8 stimulate local and distant immune responses (Induced Systemic Resistance, ISR) to 9 prevent future pathogenic attacks (Pieterse *et al.*, 2014). Priming of plant defenses is 10 marked by changes in transcriptional, metabolic, and epigenetic levels after stimulus 11 perception (Conrath, 2011; Mauch-Mani *et al.*, 2017). Upon pathogenic attack, the host 12 plant (under a primed state) is able to mount a quicker and/or robust immune response 13 that defines the post-challenge outcome of the plant-pathogen interaction. As a result of 14 this enhanced response, plants can effectively ward off pathogenic infections and/or 15 develop stress tolerance. Priming has also been reported to be durable along the plant's 16 life cycle, and transmitted to the following plant generations in some sort of plant 17 immunological memory manner (De Medeiros *et al.*, 2017; Mauch-Mani *et al.*, 2017). 18 The molecular and physiological mechanisms underlying the outcome of priming and 19 ISR are currently viewed as the central strategies used by *Trichoderma* spp. to 20 counteract disease development in distant tissues (Pieterse *et al.*, 2014). However, the 21 nature of the ISR activated by *Trichoderma* spp. is still under discussion. Several 22 studies have shown that some *Trichoderma* strains are able to elicit ISR in a Jasmonic

23 acid (JA)- and Ethylene (ET)-dependent form in leaves of dicot plants (Contreras-

24 Cornejo *et al.*, 2011; Salas-Marina *et al.*, 2011; Hermosa *et al.*, 2012); whereas others

25 have concluded that *Trichoderma* might trigger systemic acquired resistance (SAR)-like

1 responses in a salicylic acid (SA)-dependent form, mainly because of the up-regulation 2 of pathogenesis-related genes (PR1, PR2, and PR5) (Hermosa *et al.*, 2012; Mathys *et*  3 *al.*, 2012). *Trichoderma*-mediated induction of the SA, ET or JA pathways within a 4 single plant seems to indicate a rather intricate mechanism for resistance activation. The 5 connection between SAR and ISR pathways may depend on the nature of the pathogen 6 and the infected tissue (Korolev *et al.*, 2008; Pieterse *et al.*, 2012).

7 Among the molecular mechanisms underlying the physiological changes induced by 8 *Trichoderma* in plants, leucine-rich repeat proteins and MAPK cascades have been 9 shown to participate in the activation of the immune system (Shoresh *et al.*, 2010). 10 Further studies have shown that, in the *Arabidopsis*-*Trichoderma*-*Pseudomonas* 11 tripartite system, the transcription factor AtMYB72 acts as a node for signal 12 convergence during the signaling events responsible for ISR activation (Segarra *et al.*, 13 2009). In addition, the redox-dependent regulator NONEXPRESSOR OF PR GENES1 14 (NPR1) (known as a transcriptional co-activator of several pathogenesis-related genes 15 in a SA-dependent mechanism) has also been involved in the JA/ET signaling events 16 responsible for ISR activation in *Arabidopsis* during the interaction between plant 17 growth-promoting rhizobacteria and plant growth-promoting fungi (Pieterse *et al.*, 18 2012).

19 Several studies on the effects of *Trichoderma* on monocots have been performed using 20 maize as the host plant. The study by Djonović et al. (2007), for example, described that 21 ISR-like responses triggered by *T. virens* in maize plants involves mainly JA/ET and 22 green leaf volatile signaling events. On the other hand, Ferrigo et al. (2014) 23 demonstrated that inoculation of maize roots with *T. harzianum* consistently reduces the 24 disease symptoms caused by *F. verticillioides*. Gene expression studies have shown that 25 SA and JA/ET are key players in systemic immunity, and suggested that the heightened

1 immune responses are due to an improved ability of the plant to detect the fungal 2 pathogen. Similar results have also been reported by Saravanakumar et al. (2016), who 3 demonstrated that *T. harzianum* is able to locally activate the maize root defense system 4 by secreting secreted cellulases, a phenomenon that is communicated to systemic tissues 5 through JA/ET signaling events and results in a reduction of curvularia leaf spot 6 disease.

7 Due to the lack of comprehensive studies related to ISR and disease resistance in maize, 8 in this work we sought to further investigate the transcriptional reprogramming in silk 9 tissues from maize plants inoculated with *Trichoderma* and challenged with *F.*  10 *verticillioides*. The main goal of our research was to focus on early events (24 h after 11 infection) of defense activation in plants subjected to priming during the whole crop 12 cycle (approximately 2 months).

13 This study allowed the evaluation of the cellular reprogramming and regulatory events 14 related to ISR in maize silks to counteract ear infection by *F. verticillioides*. Overall, the 15 dataset obtained also provides critical information for a better understanding of 16 hormonal and metabolic signals in distinct tissues from maize plants.

17 18

## 1 **Results**

## 2 *Maize inbred line B73/ T. atroviride/ F. verticilliodes as the model study system*

3 It is well-known that different species of *Trichoderma* can induce varying intensities of 4 the host plant immune response. As mentioned above, in this study, we evaluated the 5 systemic responses induced in maize by four reference strains of *Trichoderma* (*T.*  6 *atroviride* IMI206040, *T. harzianum*, *T. reesei* Q6 and *T. virens* Gv-29-8). Before the 7 evaluation of immune responses, *Trichoderma* was confirmed to be confined to plant 8 roots, and thus, any differential effect detected in leaves would be the result of the 9 systemic responses induced after root colonization (**Supplementary Fig. S1**).

10 The first approach was to determine the synthesis of antifungal compounds in leaves in 11 *Trichoderma*-root-inoculated maize plants. In this context, the *F. verticillioides* growth 12 rate was evaluated on PDA plates in the presence of methanolic extracts prepared from 13 maize plants. Colonies of *F. verticillioides* were compared 24 h after inoculation, and 14 the fungal growth rate in extracts from *Trichoderma*-inoculated plants showed a 15 reduction of up to 50% as compared to that in extracts from control plants (**Fig. 1**). This 16 reduction was paralleled by differential accumulation of anthocyanins and flavonoids 17 (data not shown). After a careful evaluation of the results, we established maize inbred 18 line B73/ *T. atroviride*/ *F. verticilliodes* as our model system.

## 19 *RNA-seq sequencing and reads mapping to the maize genome*

20 The ability of some *Trichoderma* species to activate local and systemic resistance 21 processes in maize has been previously reported (Ferrigo *et al.*, 2014). Here, 22 transcriptional studies on *T. atroviride*-root-inoculated maize plants were conducted 24 23 h after *F. verticillioides* infection to further investigate the distant activation of plant 24 immune responses. To better understand plant treatments, **Figure 2a** summarizes maize 25 growth and inoculation/infection protocol. Four different conditions were analyzed by

1 RNA-seq: control with no fungal treatment (TmFm), *T. atroviride*-inoculated (TpFm), 2 *F. verticillioides*-infected (TmFp) and, *F. verticilliodes*-infected plus *T. atroviride*-3 inoculated (TpFp) plants (**Table 1**). Overall, almost 350 million 100-bp reads were 4 obtained, showing on average high sequencing quality (Phred scores > 30). The 5 alignment rate to the maize reference genome ranged from 67% to 79%, being 73% on 6 average. Except for sample TpFp3, all samples showed uniform values regarding 7 mapping statistics. On average, 48% of the mapped reads were successfully assigned to 8 known protein-coding transcripts as annotated in the *Z. mays* B73 reference genome 9 (**Supplementary Table S1**). The correlation chart and dendogram, based on global 10 levels of expression, showed that the variability among samples was mainly explained 11 by the infection with *F. verticillioides* **(Supplementary Fig. S2)**.

#### 12 *Maize differentially expressed (DE) genes*

13 Three sets of significantly DE genes (adjusted *p*-value < 0.05 and log2FC > |1.5|) were 14 identified: genes that responded to *F. verticillioides* infection (TmFp/TmFm), genes that 15 responded to *T. atroviride* inoculation (TpFm/TmFm), and genes that responded to both 16 *F. verticillioides* infection and *T. atroviride* inoculation (TpFp/TmFm) (**Supplementary**  17 **Text**). A total of 652 genes were identified as DE in at least one of the three contrasts 18 (**Supplementary Table S1**): 332 were identified in TmFp/TmFm (191 up-regulated and 19 141 down-regulated); 559 in TpFp/TmFm (310 up-regulated and 249 down-regulated); 20 and 5 in TpFm/TmFm (3 up-regulated and 2 down-regulated) (**Fig. 2**).

21 A global analysis using the Venn Diagram (**Fig. 2c**) indicated that only two out of all 22 the DE genes (GRMZM2G125747 and GRMZM2G394203) were common to all the 23 contrasts, and that 240 were shared between TmFp/TmFm and TpFp/TmFm. A detailed 24 analysis and conclusions for each situation are provided as **Supplementary Text**.

1 After transcriptional profiling, RNA-seq results were validated by qRT-PCR, using a set 2 of ten genes identified as differentially regulated in the comparisons described earlier. 3 Overall, the expression profiles determined by qRT-PCR displayed trends similar to 4 those determined by RNA-seq for up-regulated genes (**Fig. 3**). However, the difference 5 in expression patterns for down-regulated genes may be related to mRNA stability.

# 6 *Early transcriptional changes after pathogen challenge in maize silks under a primed*

#### 7 *state of defenses*

8 The expression data of the DE genes were surveyed by cluster analysis, and genes were 9 grouped into four clusters based on their expression behavior (**Fig. 4**). *Cluster 1*  10 grouped genes displaying similar log2FC values in TmFp and TpFp treatments 11 compared to control plants (included in TmFp/TmTm and TpFp/TmFm lists, 12 respectively) but not displaying DE in *T. atroviride*-only inoculated plants (those in the 13 TpFm/TmFm gene list); *Cluster 2* grouped genes displaying greater log2FC values in 14 TpFp/TmFm than in TmFp/TmFm; *Cluster 3* grouped genes exclusively detected as DE 15 after *F. verticilliodes* infection (TmFp/TmFm); and *Cluster 4* grouped genes exclusively 16 detected as DE in TpFp plants (TpFp/TmFm gene list).

17 Based on this analysis, we focused on the 339 genes regulated by priming, which were 18 those grouped in Clusters 2 and 4. Thus, this classification included all the genes 19 exclusively activated/deactivated in TpFp plants, as well as those with a significant DE 20 when comparing the gene expression in TpFp plants versus that in TmFp plants 21 (Mathys *et al.*, 2012; Perazzolli *et al.*, 2012; Pieterse *et al.*, 2014). This behavior 22 indicates an enhancement of maize immunological responses elicited by *Trichoderma* 23 pre-treatment. The whole set of genes identified as priming-related genes is detailed in 24 **Supplementary Table S2**.

1 Transcriptomic analysis using MapMan 3.6.0 software (Thimm *et al.*, 2004) allowed the 2 functional classification of DE genes (**Fig. 5**). Accordingly, the most represented 3 categories corresponded to cell structure/cell wall, amino acid and protein metabolism, 4 response to stress, signaling, and transport (8.5%, 7.5%, 6%, 9.5% and 13% 5 respectively). It is important to highlight that 10.5% and 23% of the total priming genes 6 (339 genes) corresponded to miscellaneous or unknown functions, respectively.

7

#### 8 *Specific gene groups differentially expressed in response to ISR*

9 *Primary metabolism.* Relatively few genes involved in primary metabolism 10 displayed significant differences (**Fig. 6a**). Down-regulation of the following genes was 11 evidenced when comparing the transcriptional profile of TpFp/TmFm versus that of 12 TmFp/TmFm: *UTP-glucose-1-phosphate uridylyltransferase 1* (GRMZM2G098370), 13 *pyrophosphate:fructose-6-phosphate 1-phosphotransferase* (GRMZM2G042502), a 14 *dihydrolipoamide acetyltransferase* coding for a component of pyruvate dehydrogenase 15 complex (GRMZM2G058702), a *plastid ADP glucose pyrophosphorylase large subunit* 16 (GRMZM2G391936), and various starch synthesis-related genes. This response was 17 likely due to reprogramming of cell carbon partitioning between storage and energy 18 production to efficiently counteract pathogen invasion upon ISR activation.

19 *Secondary metabolism*. Transcriptional changes in secondary metabolism are of 20 major interest since they are either directly or indirectly involved in the synthesis of 21 antimicrobial compounds. In this category, the analysis was focused on the biosynthesis 22 of waxes, terpenes, flavonoids, and phenylpropanoids. Several genes involved in the 23 biosynthesis of waxes and terpenes, such as *glossy1* (GRMZM2G114642) and *terpene*  24 *synthase* (GRMZM2G010356), were up-regulated by ISR. However, a gene coding for 25 cinnamyl-alcohol dehydrogenase (GRMZM2G046070), an enzyme responsible for the

1 final step in a branch of the phenylpropanoid pathway, specific for the production of 2 lignin monomers, was down-regulated. In addition, the *chalcone synthase* profile was 3 expected to be modified as evidenced by the down-regulation of GRMZM2G151227. 4 The evidence also demonstrated that a specific reprogramming of terpene and flavonoid 5 metabolisms occurred in silk cells. It seems that, to produce a more effective battery of 6 antimicrobial and signaling compounds, silks require a specific and tailored synthesis of 7 secondary metabolites during ISR. It is likely that specific secondary metabolites are 8 produced to counteract *F. verticillioides* in maize cells under an immunological 9 alertness state.

10 *Plant cell wall.* During pathogen attack, the cell wall integrity is compromised 11 and one of the responses is the reinforcement of the cell-wall structure. Genes coding 12 for a bifunctional UDP-glucose 4-epimerase and an UDP-xylose 4-epimerase 1 13 (GRMZM2G052336), involved in the biosynthesis of colanic acid precursors, were up-14 regulated under priming conditions. In contrast, many genes, such as those coding for a 15 putative mannan synthase (GRMZM2G099088), a putative cellulose synthase-like 16 family protein (GRMZM2G015886) and a xyloglucan endotransglucosylase/hydrolase 17 protein (ac210669.3\_fgt001), the latter of which catalyzes transfer reactions between 18 xyloglucans and soluble cellulosic substrates, were down-regulated. We also identified 19 two genes coding for β-D-xylosidase (GRMZM2G136895 and GRMZM2G111998) and 20 three genes coding for expansins (GRMZM2G105844, GRMZM2G056236, 21 GRMZM2G082520), which were down-regulated. These proteins are important for cell 22 wall loosening and recycling during plant growth. These observations would be in line 23 with a decrease in the enzymes involved in the biosynthesis of structural 24 polysaccharides and a general metabolic reorganization, likely to reinforce a physical 25 barrier to prevent pathogenic invasion.

1 *Defense genes.* Most of the genes related to biotic stress detected as ISR-related, 2 particularly, several genes coding for pathogenesis-related proteins such as PR-1 3 (GRMZM2G304442), PR-5 (GRMZM2G402631), thaumatin family proteins 4 (GRMZM2G092474, GRMZM2G136372), protein P21 (GRMZM2G039639), stress-5 induced protein 1 (GRMZM2G374971), chitinase 1 (GRMZM2G358153), 6 endochitinase A (GRMZM2G051943), acidic endochitinase (GRMZM2G133781), 7 win1 (GRMZM2G117989), xylanase inhibitor protein 1 (GRMZM2G328171), and 8 glucan endo-β-1,3-glucanase (GRMZM2G125032), were up-regulated (**Fig. 6b**). This 9 confirms that the primary defensive response of maize silks is to counteract pathogenic 10 invasion by releasing an enzymatic arsenal against *Fusarium* cell wall for two reasons: 11 i) to attempt to prevent pathogen development and ii) to reinforce host defenses by the 12 release of MAMPS/PAMPS after pathogen cell-wall degradation.

13 *Redox metabolism*. It is known that redox metabolism plays an important role in 14 the immune response in plants. Among the genes classified as ISR-responsive, two 15 genes coding for peroxidase were modified: one was up-regulated (ac205154.3\_fgt005), 16 whereas the other down-regulated (GRMZM2G023840). Also, the gene coding for 17 APx3-peroxisomal ascorbate peroxidase (GRMZM2G004211) was down-regulated**.**  18 Three genes coding for glutathione transferase were observed: two of them 19 (GRMZM2G119499 and GRMZM2G096269) were down-regulated, whereas the other 20 (GRMZM2G032856) was up-regulated. Also, GRMZM2G135385, which codes for a 21 cytochrome b5, was up-regulated in response to priming in silk cells. These findings are 22 in line with the role of glutathione and reactive oxygen species (ROS) at early stages of 23 maize-fungal interaction (Vargas *et al.*, 2012). Overall, these results demonstrate a 24 strong reorganization of redox homeostasis in maize silks under a primed state in 25 response to a pathogenic attack.

1 *Transcription factors.* An important outcome of this transcriptional analysis was 2 the identification of several transcription factors differentially expressed as a 3 consequence of ISR. These regulatory elements play critical roles in the set up of the 4 transcriptional reprogramming and the immune response enhancement after the 5 pathogenic attack in primed cells. Several types of transcriptional factors, including 6 Aux/IAA, basic-domain leucine-zipper (bZIP), ethylene-responsive-element-binding 7 factors (ERF), MYB and WRKY, have been reported to be involved in plant-pathogen 8 interactions (Singh et al., 2002; Eulgem and Somssich, 2007; Kazan and Manners, 9 2009). However, ISR responses in maize silks also included specific changes in genetic 10 elements involved in gene expression regulation. For instance, the expression of a gene 11 coding for an ethylene-responding AP2-EREBP-transcription factor 12 (GRMZM2G085678) was down-regulated (**Fig. 6b**), while three genes coding for 13 Aux/IAA, bZIP and WRKY transcription factors (GRMZM2G001799, 14 GRMZM2G073427, and GRMZM2G040298, respectively) were up-regulated during 15 the ISR activation.

16 *Receptor kinases.* RNA-Seq results and priming-related gene analysis allowed 17 detecting specific receptor kinases that likely act as cell-wall pathogen sensors, such as 18 Leucine-Rich Receptor (LRR), DUF26, and Cysteine-rich Receptor (CRK) (Wrzaczek 19 *et al.*, 2010). These membrane-associated kinases play critical roles during signal 20 perception and activation of the immune system. The gene list presented in 21 **Supplementary Table S2** demonstrates that specific brassinosteroid-related signaling 22 events are up-regulated during ISR responses, as evidenced by the induction of a co-23 receptor associated with Leucine-rich repeat RLKs (LRR-RLKs) (*BRASSINOSTEROID*  24 *INSENSITIVE 1-associated receptor kinase 1*, GRMZM2G015933, BAK1), while many 25 others were down-regulated (GRMZM2G119850, GRMZM2G463904,

Molecular Plant-Microbe Interactions "First Look" paper • http://dx.doi.org/10.1094/MPMI-07-18-0208-R • posted 09/25/2018 This paper has been peer reviewed and accepted for publication but has not yet been copyedited or proofread. The final published version may differ.

1 GRMZM2G120657, GRMZM2G099981, GRMZM2G447447, GRMZM2G371137, 2 GRMZM2G145753, GRMZM2G138338). In parallel, four additional genes 3 (GRMZM2G087625, GRMZM2G140231, GRMZM2G177726, GRMZM2G340048) 4 coding for the DUF26 protein family were up-regulated in our selection of priming 5 genes. In the case of GRMZM2G087625, its expression level in TpFp/TmFm doubled 6 the log2FC value calculated in TmFp/TmFm. This observation indicates the importance 7 of the gene product in signaling transduction at the early stages of the infection in silks 8 under ISR.

9 After signal perception at the cell surface, secondary messengers (such as calcium) are 10 critical for signal amplification and transduction (Lanubile *et al.*, 2014). In this context, 11 transcripts involved in the calcium-mediated signal transduction pathways were 12 identified. Genes coding for a SF16 protein with the IQ calmodulin-binding motif 13 (GRMZM2G024799), calreticulin (GRMZM2G074687) and putative calcium-14 dependent protein kinase family protein (GRMZM2G030673) were detected as 15 differentially down-regulated when TpFp/TmFm was compared with TmFp/TmFm. This phenomenon seems to indicate that some Ca2+ 16 -induced pathways are attenuated in 17 maize silks after ISR activation.

18 *Hormone signaling.* Based on MapMan analysis, several genes related to ABA 19 and ethylene signaling were down-regulated when ISR was activated (**Fig. 6b**). 20 Particularly, a gene coding for a protein in the abscisic acid-responsive HVA22 family 21 (GRMZM2G101062) and two genes (GRMZM2G007249 and GRMZM2G013448) 22 coding for the enzyme 1-aminocyclopropane-1-carboxylate oxidase, responsible for 23 ethylene biosynthesis, displayed lower expression levels in the TpFp/TmFm condition. 24 In contrast, SA and IAA signaling events appeared to be enhanced in primed silks after 25 pathogen attack. In the evaluation of auxin-related pathways, IAA biosynthesis seemed

1 to be activated, as the gene coding for IAA amino acid hydrolase ILR1 2 (GRMZM2G016958) was up-regulated. Similarly, two genes coding for 3 salicylate/benzoate carboxyl methyltransferase (GRMZM2G010731 and 4 GRMZM2G126772), responsible for SA methyl-ester (MeSA) formation, were up-5 regulated. MeSA has been proposed to act as a volatile systemic messenger that is 6 produced at the infection site by salicylate/benzoate carboxyl methyltransferase 1 7 (BSMT1), and transduced to systemic tissues where it is converted back to SA to 8 activate defenses in non-infected parts of the plant (Park *et al.*, 2007; Gao *et al.*, 2014).

9 Finally, no genes involved in the JA signaling pathways were found among the priming-10 related genes in silk cells 24 h post-infection. It is likely that, in this tripartite system, 11 the responses mediated by JA are not fully developed at early stages of the pathogenic 12 attack and a more detailed analysis will be required.

# 13 *Analysis of SA, JA, and ABA as the main players in ISR-mediated immunity in maize* 14 *silks*

15 To gain insights into the hormonal changes occurring after each treatment, we compared 16 the levels of SA, JA and ABA in the silks of the four treatments studied (**Fig. 7**). The 17 most significant changes were observed in the SA levels (**Fig. 7a**). It is worth 18 mentioning that, at early stages of the infection process, *F. verticillioides* acts as a 19 biotrophic pathogen leading to the activation of the SA pathway (Glazebrook, 2005). 20 This is concordant with the significant increase in the levels of this hormone, detected in 21 the TmFp treatment compared to the control TmFm. TpFp presented lower SA levels 22 than TmFp and TpFm, and similar to the control condition (TmFm). This reduction in 23 SA content agrees with the increased levels of the *salicylate/benzoate carboxyl*  24 *methyltransferase* gene, whose protein product transforms SA into MeSA, a signal 25 molecule for long-distance communication.

1 Regarding JA levels, it seems that the main effect was that exerted by *F. verticillioides* 2 infection (**Fig. 7b**). In agreement with this, no significant differences in JA levels were 3 detected between TpFm and TmFm, while similar values were measured for TmFp and 4 TpFp. Therefore, *Fusarium* infection produces similar JA levels independently of the 5 *Trichoderma* treatment, which is in agreement with previous reports that no major 6 changes in JA were detected in plants expressing ISR (Pieterse *et al.*, 2000). On the 7 other hand, the analysis of ABA levels showed no significant changes (*p*-value < 0.05) 8 between TmFp, TpFm and TpFp with respect to the control TmFm (**Fig. 7c**). Based on 9 both hormone and transcriptional analyses, it seems that for the 10 *maize/Trichoderma/Fusarium* tripartite system, the priming responses in maize silks 11 may be related to the SA, and to some extent to the JA, signaling pathways.

12 To further support this concept, the transcriptional behavior of key genes involved in 13 the hormonal synthesis and signaling pathways of both SA and JA was further 14 investigated. By relaxing the criteria to consider significant DE genes those with *p*-adj < 15 0.05 but regardless the log2FC, we found 33 and 22 additional genes related to the SA 16 and JA pathways in TpFp/TmFm and TmFp/TmFm, respectively (data not shown).

17 As depicted in **Supplementary Fig. S3a**, the signaling pathway of SA was in general 18 up-regulated during ISR in maize silks. For instance, the gene coding for the master 19 regulator NPR1/NH1 (GRMZM2G077197), which that interacts with TGA 20 transcription factors (and activates plant defenses), was slightly increased in the 21 TpFp/TmFm condition. In addition, two genes coding for transcriptional factors 22 homologous to WRKY13 (GRMZM2G013391 and GRMZM2G141299) were up-23 regulated in the TpFp/TmFm condition. In *Oryza sativa* (Cheng *et al.*, 2015), WRKY13 24 works as a transcriptional repressor of WRKY45/WRKY45-1. Then, in good agreement 25 with WRKY13 up-regulation, in our tripartite system, WRK45/WRKY45-1 was down-

1 regulated in the same condition. Regarding the synthesis of SA, a gene coding for 2 isochorismate synthase (*ICS1*, GRMZM2G150616) was repressed in both the 3 TpFp/TmFm and TmFp/TmFm conditions. This enzyme is crucial in the regulation of 4 the SA pathway and it has been reported that NPR1 negatively regulates the expression 5 pathway of ICS1 by a mechanism that is still unknown (Wildermuth *et al.*, 2001; Zhang 6 *et al.*, 2010). Then, the negative regulation of ICS1, together with higher levels of 7 salicylate/benzoate carboxyl methyltransferase, is in good agreement with the lower 8 levels of SA detected in TpFp (**Fig.7a**). It is likely that, during ISR responses, SA levels 9 reach a threshold level determined by the balance between the biosynthetic rate and its 10 transformation into MeSA (Park et al., 2007). In this context, SA homeostasis would 11 play a central role in the local and distant signaling upon *F. verticillioides* infection in 12 ISR-expressing silks.

13 After a JA signaling pathway survey, an increase in the expression of the genes coding 14 for the repressor JAZ, the co-repressor TPL, and the adaptor protein NINJA was 15 evidenced in the TpFp/TmFm condition **(Supplementary Fig. S3b)**. However, in most 16 cases, the increase observed in TpFp/TmFm was greater than in the TmFp/TmFm 17 condition (**Supplementary Fig. 3b**), which could be attributed to the effect of priming. 18 These genes are known to be critical repressors of expression modulated by the MYC 19 (wounding-response genes) and ERF (pathogen-response genes) transcription factors. In 20 our case, the up-regulated levels of the MYC transcription factor would be inhibiting 21 the expression of the genes induced by the ERF transcription factor. Accordingly, the 22 gene encoded by GRMZM2G046532 (with 65.7 % identity to PDF2.1) was down-23 regulated in maize silks under ISR.

24 All expression data are available in **Supplementary Table S1**.

- 25
- 26

#### 1 **Discussion**

2 ISR is one of the most intriguing processes activated in plants when interacting with 3 beneficial microorganisms. This phenomenon has been investigated for many years, but 4 the regulatory and metabolic aspects are not yet fully understood (Conrath, 2011; 5 Mathys et al., 2012; Nawrocka and Małolepsza, 2013; Pieterse et al., 2014). To gain 6 new insights into the molecular mechanisms involved in ISR responses in plants, here 7 we defined a model system based on the worldwide importance of maize, and the strong 8 impact of *F. verticillioides* on crop yielding and food safety (Lanubile *et al.*, 2017).

9 It is widely known that no maize line is fully resistant to *Fusarium* attack; however, 10 many studies have demonstrated the beneficial effects of some *Trichoderma* species as 11 biological agents to reduce *Fusarium* infection (Ferrigo *et al.*, 2014; Saravanakumar *et*  12 *al.*, 2016). In this context, the effect of *Trichoderma* may be attributed either to a direct 13 antagonism of *Fusarium* on soil inocula or to the induction of a heightened state of 14 plant immunity (Djonović *et al.*, 2007; Ferrigo *et al.*, 2014). However, the mechanisms 15 underlying the effects of *Trichoderma* on maize are not yet fully understood. Thus, the 16 focus of this research was to globally explore the early regulatory events modulated by 17 ISR in maize silks after *F. verticillioides* infection, through a transcriptional approach.

18 Our findings allowed identifying specific responses regulated by ISR in plants primed at 19 the seedling stage and pathogen-challenged two months later (during the transition from 20 the vegetative to the reproductive stage, VT to R0-R1). Functional categories of DE 21 genes included transport and signaling, amino acid and protein metabolism, and 22 biotic/abiotic stress response (**Fig. 5**). This clearly demonstrates a mandatory and urgent 23 metabolic reorganization of plant cells to mount a quicker and more efficient 24 immunological response.

1 In good agreement with the hallmark of ISR, where the primed state in unchallenged 2 plants is often imperceptible (Pieterse *et al.*, 2014), only five genes displayed 3 differential mRNA levels (**Fig. 2c**) in silks of *T. atroviride*-inoculated plants. The 4 identification of these genes highlighted the importance of cell wall metabolism and 5 steroid-mediated signaling events in the alertness state of silks during ISR. The 6 activation of a gene coding for a cell wall structural protein is in agreement with an 7 enhanced plant cell-wall metabolism and structural reinforcement to reduce pathogen 8 ingress, one of the widely known effects of *Trichoderma* after plant root colonization 9 (Harman *et al*., 2004). The activation of a gene coding for a putative cycloartenol 10 synthase, which converts (S)-2,3-epoxysqualene into cycloartenol, also unraveled the 11 importance of steroid-based secondary metabolism in the heightened state of defense in 12 plants. Cycloartenol is an important triterpenoid of the sterol class found in plants and is 13 the starting point for the synthesis of most plant steroids (Schaller, 2003; Gas-Pascual *et*  14 *al.*, 2014). The distant and long-lasting enhancement of steroid metabolism mediated by 15 *Trichoderma* in maize is in agreement with previous reports on terpenes (and their 16 derivatives) acting as signals related to attacks by pathogens or herbivores (Raguso, 17 2009; Chen *et al.*, 2011). The systemic effect of *Trichoderma* on the secondary 18 metabolism of plants has been mainly related to the accumulation of antimicrobial 19 compounds (Druzhinina *et al.*, 2011), but the role of steroid derivatives is likely to have 20 a more specific role in the signaling and maintenance of the ISR status in distant tissues. 21 On the other hand, genes specifically regulated by ISR after pathogen challenge were 22 evidenced when comparing genes listed in the TpFp/TmFm vs the TmFp/TmFm 23 categories. The analysis of this gene core set allowed the discovery of a regulatory 24 network that might be responsible for the activation and amplification of pathogen-25 related signals in plants under a heightened state of defense. This regulatory network

Page 21 of 86

1 involves the brassinosteroid co-receptor BAK1 (specific for brassinosteroid and MAMP 2 signaling), and cysteine-rich receptors from the DUF26 family, and several transcription 3 factors.

4 BAK1 is a co-receptor acting together with BIR and other LRR receptors, which 5 mediates responses elicited by brassinosteroids or by pathogen-released PAMPS, such 6 as Flg22 (Huot *et al.*, 2014). Brassinosteroids have been defined as the sixth plant 7 hormones, with analogous structure to animal steroid hormones, which play central 8 roles in diverse aspects of plant biology, including immunity (He *et al.*, 2007; Huot *et*  9 *al.*, 2014; Tang *et al.*, 2016). Previous reports have demonstrated that, in Arabidopsis, 10 brassinosteroid signaling cascades are involved in disease resistance against 11 *Pseudomonas syringae* and the oomycete *Hyaloperonospora arabidopsidis*, in a PAMP-12 triggered immunity (PTI)-dependent mechanism (Roux *et al.*, 2011). This observation 13 suggests that, in maize silks expressing ISR, signaling events mediated by BAK1 might 14 be key players in the recognition of endogenous or pathogen-derived signal molecules. 15 Then, supporting the concept of the interplay between PTI and receptor kinases to elicit 16 immunological responses in primed plants, several DUF26 homologs were also 17 differentially regulated after pathogen challenge in maize plants under ISR. In many 18 cases, receptors belonging to the DUF26 family have been related to the activation of 19 PTI mechanisms. For instance, in *Arabidopsis* plants overexpressing the LecRK-VI.2- 20 responsive CRK4, CRK6 and CRK36, PTI mechanisms were enhanced and transgenic 21 plants developed resistance to the pathogen *Pseudomonas syringae* (Yeh *et al.*, 2015).

22 According to our gene expression analysis, a synergistic effect between brassinosteroids 23 and PAMPs seemed to be occurring in maize plants to quickly intensify the activation 24 of local immune responses during ISR. This conclusion is supported by the 25 enhancement of the steroid biosynthetic pathway, the increased expression of the co-

1 receptor BAK1, and the higher expression level of other PTI-related receptors after 2 pathogen challenge.

3 Correlating with signal perception and transduction into the host cell, the expected 4 outcome is a global physiological reorganization to cope with the stressful situation. In 5 this context, transcriptional regulators were also detected as part of the responses 6 mediated by ISR. Initial PTI responses are triggered within a few hours after PAMP 7 perception and include a ROS burst, pathogenesis-related proteins and other general 8 immune responses (Huot *et al.*, 2014). PTI-related transcriptional reprogramming has 9 been partly associated with the WRKY family of transcription factors (Pandey and 10 Somssich, 2009; Huot et al., 2014). However, the gene catalog provided in 11 **Supplemental Table S2** (ISR-modulated genes) includes a WRKY protein, together 12 with a bZIP and an Aux/IAA regulator as the main transcription factors specifically up-13 regulated after *F. verticillioides* infection of primed silks.

14 Collectively, all these results point to an intricate regulatory network activated in 15 primed cells that locally works to prevent pathogen efforts to enter the plant cell. The 16 perception of the pathogen hypha in the host cell surroundings seems to trigger three 17 immediate molecular responses in an attempt to arrest pathogenic infection. First, 18 antimicrobial compounds (such as ROS, flavonoids and other secondary metabolites) 19 are synthesized by maize cells to inhibit *Fusarium* growth inside maize cells. At the 20 same time, maize cells reorganize their main physical barrier (the cell wall) to delay 21 further pathogenic invasion. Finally, an arsenal of extracellular enzymes produced to 22 degrade *Fusarium* cell wall would assist to locally restrain pathogen growth and 23 promote the generation of massive amounts of PTI elicitors.

24 Our transcriptome analysis, together with the hormone quantitative determinations 25 performed, also shed some light on the role of hormones in the regulation of immune

| 1  | responses in maize silks after ISR activation. The most outstanding finding was an                    |
|----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 2  | apparent combination of effects mediated by SA and JA signaling pathways                              |
| 3  | (Supplementary Fig. S3). Contrary to previous studies performed in maize, or other                    |
| 4  | monocots (Djonovic et al., 2007; Contreras-Cornejo et al. 2011; Salas-Marina et al.                   |
| 5  | 2011; Hermosa et al., 2012; Pieterse et al., 2014), no evidences of the activation of                 |
| 6  | specific<br>ET-related<br>pathways<br>were<br>detected<br>in<br>infected<br>silks<br>under<br>ISR     |
| 7  | (Supplementary Table S2), and even the gene coding for the ET-biosynthetic enzyme                     |
| 8  | was down-regulated. On the other hand, based on hormone levels, it appears that SA                    |
| 9  | plays a crucial role in long-distance signaling after pathogen infection. The evidence                |
| 10 | presented shows that SA is accumulated in silks of T. atroviride-inoculated plants or                 |
| 11 | after F. verticillioides infection (Fig. 7a). However, during pathogen infection in primed            |
| 12 | plants (TpFp), the significant decrease in SA levels may be attributed to a down                      |
| 13 | regulation of the biosynthetic pathway, together with the conversion of free SA into                  |
| 14 | MeSA.<br>The<br>higher<br>levels<br>of<br>the<br>transcript<br>for<br>salicylate/benzoate<br>carboxyl |
| 15 | methyltransferase highlights that the lower levels of SA are associated with an increased             |
| 16 | synthesis of MeSA as a signal for non-infected tissues (Park et al., 2007; Kumar, 2014;               |
| 17 | Pieterse et al., 2014; Mauch-Mani et al., 2017). The regulatory pathways under the                    |
| 18 | influence of SA displayed three main changes specific to ISR and correlated to a                      |
| 19 | decrease in SA levels: the transcriptional activation of the master regulator NPR1, the               |
| 20 | activation of PR1 (a SA marker gene) and the repression of WRKY76 (Supplementary                      |
| 21 | Fig. S3 a). On the other hand, JA-mediated effects on a primed immune system were                     |
| 22 | connected to the transcriptional activation of JAZ, NINJA and EIN3/EIL1, and the                      |
| 23 | repression of a PDF2.1-like gene (GRMZM2G046532, Supplementary Fig. S3 b).                            |
| 24 | These main transcriptional changes due to ISR also affected genes coding for additional               |

1 accessory proteins, including COI1, MYC-like, and TPL, necessary to execute a global 2 gene expression program to trigger defensive responses.

3 It is likely that, in maize silks, SA acts as a central signaling element to alert distant 4 tissues during ISR responses. According to the data presented here, maize silks under a 5 primed state of defense establish a strong battle to immediately confine *F.*  6 *verticillioides*, and, at the same time, release the volatile MeSA to trigger immune 7 responses in non-infected tissues or neighbor plants.

8 Taken together, transcriptomic and hormonal studies highlighted steroids (likely 9 brassinosteroids) and SA signaling pathways as the leading actors in maize silks to 10 mediate local and systemic activation of defenses.

#### 1 **Materials and methods**

2

#### 3 *Fungal strains*

4 Four reference strains of *Trichoderma* (*T. virens* Gv29-8, *T. atroviride* IMI206040, *T.*  5 *harzianum*, and *T. reesei* Q6) and the *F. verticillioides* P364 isolate were grown on 6 potato dextrose agar (PDA; Difco) for 10 days at 25°C. Conidia were harvested by 7 gentle scraping in 2 mL sterile water, counted in a Neubauer chamber and diluted in 8 sterile water as described elsewhere (Iglesias *et al.*, 2010).

9

#### 10 *Plant material*

11 Maize (*Zea mays*, inbred line B73) seeds were surface disinfected with 100% ethanol 12 for 5 min, followed by 4 % NaClO for 15 min, washed five times with sterile distilled 13 water and germinated in a humidity chamber at 25 °C in the dark. Healthy seedlings 14 were transferred to soil and inoculated with the *Trichoderma* strains required for each 15 case.

16

## 17 *Fungal inoculation and infection of maize plants for transcriptional analysis*

18 Germinated maize seeds, with no evidence of fungal growth, were transferred to 30-L 19 pots containing sterile soil and 5 days later inoculated with 2 mL of a *T. atroviride* spore suspension (1 × 10<sup>5</sup> 20 spores/mL). The pots were transferred to the greenhouse until 21 the emergence of silks.

For pathogen inoculation, 2 mL of a conidial suspension of *F. verticillioides* (1 × 10<sup>6</sup> 22 23 conidia/mL) was injected into the silk channel approximately 4-6 days after silking, by 24 using the technology developed by Iglesias et al. (2010). The silks were collected 24 h 25 post-infection and stored at -80 ºC. The inoculation and infection protocols are 4

## 5 *RNA purification, library preparation, and RNA sequencing*

6 Total RNA was extracted from 100 mg of tissue using the Trizol protocol (Invitrogen) 7 according to the manufacturer's instructions. The amount and quality of total RNA were 8 estimated spectrophotometrically at 260 and 280 nm as well as by agarose 1 % (w/v) gel 9 electrophoresis. For quality control of RNA samples, 1 µL of each sample was run on a 10 2100 Bioanalyzer (Agilent Technologies) using the Agilent RNA 6000 Peak Kit.

11 The TruSeq® RNA Sample Preparation Guide from Illumina was used for RNA 12 processing and library construction. According to the quality controls on the 2100 13 Bioanalyze and using a DNA 1000 Kit, the libraries were composed of fragments of 14 double-stranded DNA of an average size of 260 bp. The libraries were quantified by 15 qPCR (Light Cycler 480 Roche), using the Kapa Library Quantification Kit according 16 to the manufacturer`s instructions. An equimolar pool of the libraries was prepared and 17 used for the generation of clusters in the sequencing cell.

18 RNA-seq analysis was performed by a fast sequencing run (2 lanes) generating 2x100-

- 19 bp paired-end reads in Hiseq 1500-Illumina.
- 20

## 21 *Bioinformatic analysis*

22 RNA-seq raw sequence reads were quality-checked using *FastQC* (Andrews, 2010) 23 (http://www.bioinformatics.babraham.ac.uk/projects/fastqc/). Fastq sequences showed 24 an overall good quality according to the *FastQC* standards (mean Phred score = 37). 25 Raw fastq sequences were trimmed to remove both low-quality bases and adapter

| 1  | sequences at the 3′-end of the reads. Trimming was performed using Trimmomatic v.0.3                  |
|----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 2  | (Bolger et al., 2014) with the options leading:11, trailing:11, slidingwindow:4:20 and                |
| 3  | minlen:24. Tophat v2.0.8 (Kim et al., 2013) was used to map trimmed reads to the                      |
| 4  | reference genome. The Ensembl release 27 of the Z. mays B73 (AGPv.3.27) was used                      |
| 5  | as a reference genome and the corresponding annotation file (Kersey et al., 2015).                    |
| 6  | FeatureCounts v.1.5 (Liao et al., 2013) was used to count reads at the gene level.                    |
| 7  | Multimapping and ambiguously mapped reads were not considered. A differential gene                    |
| 8  | expression analysis was performed using the DeSeq2 package (Love et al., 2014). The                   |
| 9  | fold-changes (FC) were calculated as the ratio of the normalized raw counts between                   |
| 10 | each treatment and the control condition treatment (TmFm). Thus, we identified genes                  |
| 11 | that responded to F. verticillioides infection (TmFp/TmFm), to T. atroviride inoculation              |
| 12 | (TpFm/TmFm)<br>or<br>simultaneously<br>to<br>both<br>F.<br>verticillioides<br>and<br>T.<br>atroviride |
| 13 | (TpFp/TmFm).<br>Genes with adjusted p-values < 0.05 and log2(FC) ≥  1.5  were                         |
| 14 | considered as significantly differentially expressed (DE). These DE genes were then                   |
| 15 | grouped according to their expression by comparing the behavior between these three                   |
| 16 | conditions using the k-means clustering method of the T-MeV 4.8.1 software (Saeed et                  |
| 17 | al., 2006).                                                                                           |
|    |                                                                                                       |

18 This cluster analysis allowed detecting priming genes induced by *Trichoderma* in silks 19 of maize plants (**Supplementary Table S2**). These priming genes were visualized and 20 functionally analyzed using MapMan 3.6.0 (Thimm et al., 2004)**.** 

21

## 22 *Hormone determination: abscisic acid (ABA), JA, and SA*

23 Hormones were extracted from lyophilized maize silks according to the protocol by 24 Durgbanshi et al. (2005). An extraction solution containing 50 ng of internal standards

The chromatography separation was performed with an HPLC Alliance 2695 (Waters,
Inc., CA, USA) equipped with a C18 reverse phase column (100 mm x 2.1 mm, 3-µm),
which is coupled with an electrospray ionization source, and a Triple Quadrupole Mass
Spectrometer (Quattro Ultima pt; Micromass, Manchester, UK). Data obtained were in
negative mode.

Identification and quantification were accomplished by injection of the samples in
Multiple Reaction Monitoring (MRM) mode. Data acquisition by MRM mode was
carried out by monitoring the parental ions, and their transitions were: ABA: 263> 153,
[<sup>2</sup>H<sub>6</sub>]-ABA: 268> 159, JA: 209> 59, [<sup>2</sup>H<sub>6</sub>]-JA: 215> 59, SA: 137> 93 and [<sup>2</sup>H<sub>4</sub>]-SA
141> 97.

Data were processed with MassLynx TM v. 4.1, Micromass (Manchester, UK) and the
values were determined as pmol/g of dried weight.

# **Supplementary Text**

Article title: **Long-Lasting primed state in maize plants. Salicylic acid and steroids signaling pathways as key players for the early activation of immune responses in silks.**

Authors *Romina B Agostini, Agustina Postigo, Sebastian P. Rius, Gabriel E. Rech, Valeria A. Campos-Bermudez, Walter A. Vargas* 

The following Supplementary Information is available for this article:

#### **Methods .**

#### *Plant root and leaf colonization assays*

Germinated maize seed with no evident fungal growth were transferred to 10 L pots containing sterile soil and five days later inoculated with 1 ml *T. atroviride* spore's suspension (1 x 10<sup>5</sup> spores/mL). After fifteen days root and leaf colonization were evaluated according to fungal development of different sections of tissue in PDA media.

#### *Antimicrobial compounds extraction and antifungal assays*

Maize leaf tissue (200 mg) from plants treated with the above-mentioned *Trichoderma* strains were grinded in liquid N2 to a powder, and 2 ml of methanol were added. After centrifugation at 4000 × *g* 10 min the supernatant was sterilized using a 0.2 µm filter device. The suspension obtained was used to evaluate its effect on *F. verticillioides* growth on PDA plates. Control plants (not inoculated with *Trichoderma*) were processed in the same way.

The extracts were added to 50 ml of PDA medium after being sterilized and before it solidifies. After standing time, a disc (5 mm diameter) PDA with mycelia of *F. verticilliodes* of 10 days old was placed in center Petri Dish and was incubated at 28±2ºC for 15 days. Daily was measuring mold growth with Vernier, the values were transforms to mycelia growth inhibition percentage. For each concentration were established three replicates and a control.

Alternatively, to evaluate the *F. verticilliodes* growth inhibition of each leaf extract whose roots were treated with *Trichoderma*, we mix in a ratio 1:1 the leaf extracts with a 1 × 10<sup>5</sup> *Fusarium* conidial suspension. Ten ml of each mix were placed onto a PDA plate. *Fusarium* growth inhibition was compared to the growth obtained for the control extracts.

#### *qRT-PCR expression analysis*

cDNAs were synthesized using 4 µg of silks RNAs and oligo (dT) with Superscript III reverse transcriptase (Invitrogen), according to the manufacturer's instructions, and used for qRT-PCR protocols.

Gene expression data from RNA-seq were validated from silk samples by quantitative reverse transcriptase (qRT)-PCR analysis. This was performed on a subset of selected genes that were up- or down-regulated during the different treatments according to RNA-seq analysis. Genespecific primers were designed along the last exon and the 3´UTR region, using Primer3 software (http://frodo.wi.mit.edu/cgi457bin/primer3/prime3 www.cgi) and their sequences are shown in Table S3.

Twenty ng of single strand cDNA were used for qRT-PCR. SYBR Green PCR Master Mix (Invitrogen) was used for the PCRs according to the manufacturer's protocol, with 0.5X SYBR Green I; PCR Buffer 1X, 2.5 mM MgCl2; 200 µM dNTPs; 0.25 µM of each primer and 0.5 U Taq Platinum Polymerase (Invitrogen). PCR reactions were performed in a Stratagene Mx3000P QPCR System with the following cycling parameters: initial denaturation at 94°C for 2 min; 45 cycles of 94°C for 15 s, 58°C for 15 s; and 72°C for 30 s. Relative quantification was normalized using eukaryotic Initiation Factor 4A (eIF4A, GRMZM2G027995) as internal control, and the expression ratio and Fold-Change (FC) were calculated using the 2<sup>−</sup>∆∆Ct method (Livak and Schmittgen, 2001). The gene expression was then normalized to the control condition; each assay was performed in triplicate, and repeated at least three times using different samples.

### **Results**

## *Reprograming of gene expression profiles in maize after fungal treatments*

## *Five genes detected as differentially regulated in Trichoderma-inoculated plants*.

The accuracy and sensibility of RNA-seq assays allowed for the identification of the gene set that remained differentially regulated in distant silks two months after root inoculation with *T. atroviride*. In this study, five genes were found as differentially regulated in silks of *Trichoderma* root-inoculated maize plants (TpFm/TmFm) (Table S1). The differentially expressed genes include the down-regulation of MYB transcription factor (GRMZM2G001875) and GRMZM2G420732 with a domain of unknown function DUF3506; and the up-regulation of cycloartenol synthase (GRMZM2G100060) from terpene and phytoesterol metabolisms, GRMZM2G394203 coding for a cell wall structural protein and the unknown GRMZM2G125747.

## *Transcriptional responses against F. verticillioides attack (TmFp/TmFm)*

The differentially expressed genes during *F. verticillioides* infection (TmFp/TmFm), are in full agreement with previous reports describing maize responses to *Fusarium* infection (Campos-Bermudez *et al.*, 2013; Lanubile *et al.*, 2017; Shu *et al.*, 2017). The dataset presented in Table S1, includes gene families commonly regulated in response to pathogenic attack, including several PR proteins, signaling cascades and transcriptional regulators. For instance, three chitinase-coding genes (GRMZM2G453805, GRMZM2G358153 and GRMZM2G005633) likely to be involved in pathogen cell-wall degradation, were up-regulated, as well as several pathogenesis-related proteins such as PR-STH-21 (GRMZM2G075283), PR-PRMS (AC205274.3\_FG001) and PR-10 (GRMZM2G112488, GRMZM2G112538, GRMZM2G112524). Other defense related proteins detected during *Fusarium* infection include a peroxidase (PRX, GRMZM2G135108), a phenylalanine ammonia lyase (PAL, GRMZM2G118345), a major pollen allergen Car b 1 (GRMZM6G175135) and a putative flavonol synthase (GRMZM2G382569).

An important event at early stages of plant pathogen interactions is the recognition of the infective pathogen. One of the main mechanisms for that is the recognition of MAMPs by specific membrane receptors (pattern recognition receptors, PRRs) such as receptor-like kinases (RLKs), and receptor-like proteins (Boutrot & Zipfel, 2017). Several PRRs, including, leucine-

rich receptor-like kinase (LRRK), RLK, serine threonine kinase (STK), brassinosteroid insensitive 1-associated receptor kinase 1 (BAK1) encoded by GRMZM2G328785, GRMZM2G155837, GRMZM2G145440, GRMZM2G359986, GRMZM2G132212, GRMZM2G332107 (respectively), were up-regulated in maize silks at early stages of *Fusarium* infection, and might be induced for the primary recognition of the pathogen.

Five transcription factors were also differentially expressed in maize silks 24 h after *Fusarium*  infection. Myb-like DNA binding proteins (GRMZM2G104789 and GRMZM2G069325), ethylene-responsive factor-like protein (GRMZM2G053503) and putative AP2/EREBP transcription factors (GRMZM2G020054, GRMZM2G129674).

The gene list also includes several unknown proteins that might be also critical for pathogen recognition and relevant for the outcome on the interaction.

*Maize ISR response during Fusarium infection in Trichoderma pretreated plants (TpFp/TmFm).* Despite that the long-lasting *Trichoderma*-maize association (TpFm/TmFm) does not cause a massive change in gene expression profile, plants pretreated with *T. atroviride* (TpFp/TmFm) exhibited a massive gene expression reprogramming 24 h after *Fusarium* infection. This reprogramming includes responses triggered by *T. atroviride*, host-pathogen specific responses, and more importantly those contributed as result of ISR activation, such as genes coding for several defense-related proteins and elements for pathogen perception and signaling cascades (Supporting InformationTable S1).

a)- Salicylic acid signaling. b)- Jasmonic acid signaling. The colored circles represent the transcriptional changes of RNA-seq analysis with p-adj < 0.05, upregulated (green) and downregulated (red) genes. The yellow circles there are not a significance level of 5%. The first circle corresponds to TpFp/TmFm condition, the second to TpFm/TmFm and the third to TmFp/TmFm condition.
